**Romania**[^cite_note-10] is a country in [Central](5188.md) and [Southeastern Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southeastern_Europe). It lies on the lower course of the [Danube](21209639.md), north of the [Balkan Peninsula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Balkan_Peninsula), and on the northwestern shore of the [Black Sea](3386.md). It borders [Ukraine](31750.md) to the north and east, [Hungary](13275.md) to the west, [Serbia](29265.md) to the southwest, [Bulgaria](3415.md) to the south, [Moldova](19260.md) to the east, and the Black Sea to the southeast. It is the [twelfth-largest country in Europe by area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_European_countries_by_area), covering 238,397 km<sup>2</sup> (92,046 mi<sup>2</sup>), and the sixth-most populous [member state of the European Union](10890716.md), with 19 million inhabitants. The capital, [largest city](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_cities_and_towns_in_Romania) and [economic centre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economy_of_Romania) is [Bucharest](36877.md). Other major cities include [Cluj-Napoca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cluj-Napoca), [Iași](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ia%C8%99i), [Constanța](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constan%C8%9Ba), [Timișoara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timi%C8%99oara), [Brașov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bra%C8%99ov) and [Craiova](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Craiova).

Romania was settled during the [Lower Paleolithic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lower_Paleolithic), later becoming [Dacia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dacia) before [Trajan's Dacian Wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trajan's_Dacian_Wars) and [Romanisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romanization_\(cultural\)). The modern Romanian state was formed in 1859 with the [unification of Moldavia and Wallachia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unification_of_Moldavia_and_Wallachia) under [Alexandru Ioan Cuza](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexandru_Ioan_Cuza), becoming the [Kingdom of Romania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Romania) in 1881 under [Carol I of Romania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carol_I_of_Romania). Romania [gained independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romanian_War_of_Independence) from the [Ottoman Empire](22278.md) in 1877, formalised by the [Treaty of Berlin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Berlin_\(1878\)). After [World War I](4764461.md), [Transylvania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transylvania), [Banat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Banat), [Bukovina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bukovina), and [Bessarabia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bessarabia) joined the [Old Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romanian_Old_Kingdom), forming [Greater Romania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greater_Romania), which reached its largest territorial extent. In 1940, under [Axis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Axis_powers) pressure, Romania lost territories to [Hungary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Hungary), [Bulgaria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Bulgaria), and the [Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Union). Following the [Act of 23 August](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1944_Romanian_coup_d'%C3%A9tat), Romania switched sides to join [the Allies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allies_of_World_War_II). After [World War II](32927.md), it regained [Northern Transylvania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_Transylvania) through the [Paris Peace Treaties](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paris_Peace_Treaties). Under [Soviet occupation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_occupation_of_Romania), [King Michael I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/King_Michael_I) was [forced to abdicate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_I_of_Romania#Forced_abdication), and Romania became a [socialist republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Socialist_Republic_of_Romania) and [Warsaw Pact](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Warsaw_Pact) member. After the [fall of communism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fall_of_communism) and the [Romanian revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romanian_revolution) in 1989, Romania transitioned to [liberal democracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberal_democracy).

Romania is a [developed country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Developed_country) with a [high-income economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/High-income_economy) and is widely regarded as a [middle power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_power) in [international relations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_relations_of_Romania). The country possesses [11 UNESCO World Heritage Sites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_World_Heritage_Sites_in_Romania). Romania is a [global net exporter of automotive parts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_vehicle_exports) and is an increasingly prominent [technology centre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_technology_centers) with some of the [fastest internet speeds in the world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_Internet_connection_speeds).&#x20;

Romania is a member of many [international organizations](18201224.md), [institutions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Institution), and forms of [political](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Politics) and [socio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sociology)-[economic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economy) [cooperation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cooperation), including: the [UN](31769.md) since 1955, [FAO](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Food_and_Agriculture_Organization) since 1961, the [Latin Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latin_Union) since 1980, the [Council of Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Council_of_Europe) since 1993, the [OSCE](22796.md) and [OIF](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Organisation_internationale_de_la_Francophonie) since 2003, [NATO](21133.md) since 29 March 2004, the [EU](9317.md) since 1 January 2007, the [Schengen free-movement area](16565139.md) toward the [Occident](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Occident) since 1 January 2025, the [EU–Mercosur free trade area](61184322.md) since 2026, etc.; and of [economic institutions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Institutional_economics): the [World Bank Group](36752.md), the [IMF](15251.md) since 1972, the [EBRD](37963.md) since 1991, the [European Investment Bank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Investment_Bank) since 2007, the [Asian Infrastructure Investment Bank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asian_Infrastructure_Investment_Bank) since 2018, etc.; and is a candidate for accession to the [OECD](33853117.md).[^cite_note-11][^cite_note-12]

## Etymology

Main article: [Name of Romania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Name_of_Romania)

*Romania* derives from the local name for Romanian ([Romanian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romanian_language): *român*), which in turn derives from [Latin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latin) [*romanus*](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/romanus), meaning "[Roman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_people)" or "of [Rome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Rome)".[^cite_note-13] This ethnonym for Romanians is first attested in the sixteenth century by Italian humanists travelling in [Transylvania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transylvania), [Moldavia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moldavia), and [Wallachia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wallachia).[^cite_note-14][^cite_note-15][^cite_note-16] The oldest known surviving document written in [Romanian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romanian_language) that can be precisely dated, a 1521 letter known as the "[Letter of Neacșu from Câmpulung](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neac%C8%99u's_letter)",[^cite_note-17] is notable for including the first documented occurrence of *Romanian* in a country name: Wallachia is mentioned as *Țara Rumânească*.

## History

Main article: [History of Romania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Romania)

Further information: [Timeline of Romanian history](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_Romanian_history) and [Origin of the Romanians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Origin_of_the_Romanians)

### Prehistory

Human remains found in [Peștera cu Oase](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pe%C8%99tera_cu_Oase) ("Cave with Bones"), radiocarbon date from circa 40,000 years ago, and represent the oldest known *[Homo sapiens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Homo_sapiens)* in Europe.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPrice201360–61-18] [Neolithic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neolithic) agriculture spread after the arrival of a mixed group of people from [Thessaly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thessaly) in the 6th millennium BC.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGeorgescu19911–2-19][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPrice2013125–127-20] Excavations at the [Poiana Slatinei](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Poiana_Slatinei?action=edit\&redlink=1) site near a [salt spring](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salt_spring) next to [Lunca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/V%C3%A2n%C4%83tori-Neam%C8%9B), Neamț yielded the earliest evidence for salt exploitation in Europe; here salt production began between the 5th and 4th millennium BC.[^cite_note-21] The first permanent settlements developed into "proto-cities",[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPrice2013149-22] which were larger than 320 hectares (800 acres).[^cite_note-23][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGeorgescu19912-24] The [Cucuteni–Trypillia culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cucuteni%E2%80%93Trypillia_culture)—the best known [archaeological culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Archaeological_culture) of [Old Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_Europe_\(archaeology\))—flourished in [Muntenia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muntenia), southeastern Transylvania and northeastern Moldavia in the 3rd millennium BC.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGeorgescu19912-24] The first fortified settlements appeared around 1800 BC, showing the militant character of [Bronze Age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bronze_Age) societies.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGeorgescu19912-24]

### Antiquity

Main article: [Romania in Antiquity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romania_in_Antiquity)

See also: [Legacy of the Roman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Legacy_of_the_Roman_Empire)



Maximum territorial extent of the [Kingdom of Dacia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dacia) during [Burebista](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Burebista)'s reign (early 40s BC)

Greek colonies established on the [Black Sea](3386.md) coast in the 7th century BC became important centres of commerce with the local tribes.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGeorgescu19913-25][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHitchins20143-26] Among the native peoples, [Herodotus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Herodotus) listed the [Getae](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Getae) of the Lower Danube region, the [Agathyrsi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agathyrsi) of Transylvania and the [Syginnae](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syginnae) of the plains along the river [Tisza](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tisza) at the beginning of the 5th century BC.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERustoiu200532,_35–36-27] Centuries later, [Strabo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Strabo) associated the Getae with the [Dacians](100550.md) who dominated the lands along the southern [Carpathian Mountains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carpathian_Mountains) in the 1st century BC.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHitchins20147-28] [Burebista](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Burebista) was the first Dacian ruler to unite the local tribes.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHitchins20147-28][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPop199914-29] He also conquered the Greek colonies in [Dobruja](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dobruja) and the neighbouring peoples as far as the Middle Danube and the [Balkan Mountains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Balkan_Mountains) between around 55 and 44 BC.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHitchins20147-28][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPop199914–15-30] After Burebista was murdered in 44 BC, his kingdom collapsed.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHitchins20147-28][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGeorgescu19914-31]

The Romans reached [Dacia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dacia) during Burebista's reign and conquered Dobruja in 46 AD.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGeorgescu19914-31] Dacia was again united under [Decebalus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Decebalus) around 85 AD.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHitchins20147-28][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPop199915-32] He resisted the Romans for decades, but the Roman army defeated his troops in 106 AD.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHitchins20149–10-33] Emperor [Trajan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trajan) transformed [Banat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Banat), [Oltenia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oltenia) and the greater part of Transylvania into a new [province](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_province) called [Roman Dacia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Dacia), but Dacian, [Germanic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Germanic_peoples) and [Sarmatian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sarmatian) tribes continued to dominate the lands along the Roman frontiers.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGeorgescu19916-34][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEOpreanu200568–69,_97–98-35] The Romans pursued an organised colonisation policy, and the provincials enjoyed a long period of peace and prosperity in the 2nd century.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPop199922–23-36][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHitchins201413–14-37] Scholars accepting the Daco-Roman continuity theory—one of the main theories about the [origin of the Romanians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Origin_of_the_Romanians)—say that the cohabitation of the native [Dacians](100550.md) and the [Roman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Empire) colonists in Roman Dacia was the first phase of the Romanians' [ethnogenesis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnogenesis).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGeorgescu199110-38][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPop199919–20-39] The [Carpians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carpians), [Goths](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Goths) and other neighbouring tribes made regular raids against Dacia from the 210s.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHitchins201414-40] The Romans could not resist, and Emperor [Aurelian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aurelian) ordered the evacuation of the province [Dacia Trajana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dacia_Trajana) in the 270s.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEOpreanu2005105–107-41] Scholars supporting the continuity theory are convinced that most Latin-speaking commoners stayed behind when the army and civil administration was withdrawn.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGeorgescu19918,_10-42] The Romans did not abandon their fortresses along the northern banks of the [Lower Danube](21209639.md) for decades, and [Dobruja](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dobruja) (known as [Scythia Minor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scythia_Minor)) remained an integral part of the Roman Empire until the early 7th century.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGeorgescu199110-38][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEOpreanu2005108,_110–111-43]

### Middle Ages

Main articles: [Romania in the Early Middle Ages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romania_in_the_Early_Middle_Ages), [Romania in the Middle Ages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romania_in_the_Middle_Ages), [Founding of Wallachia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_of_Wallachia), and [Founding of Moldavia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Founding_of_Moldavia)



Gutthiuda, or the land of the [Gothic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gothic_language)-speaking [Thervingi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thervingi), and the neighbouring tribes (370s AD)

The Goths were expanding towards the Lower Danube from the 230s, forcing the native peoples to flee to the [Roman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Empire) or to accept their [suzerainty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suzerainty).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHitchins201416-44][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHeather2010116–117,_165-45][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEOpreanu2005117–118-46] The Goths' rule ended abruptly when the [Huns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Huns) invaded their territory in 376, causing new waves of migrations.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHitchins201416-44][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEOpreanu2005117–118-46][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHeather2010151-47] The Huns forced the remnants of the local population into submission, but their empire collapsed in 454.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHitchins201416-44][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHeather2010151,_207–208-48] The [Gepids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gepids) took possession of the former Dacia province.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBóna199466–67-49][^cite_note-FOOTNOTECurta200654-50] Place names that are of [Slavic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slavic_languages) origin abound in Romania, indicating that a significant Slavic-speaking population lived in the territory.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEOpreanu2005131-51] The first [Slavic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_Slavs) groups settled in [Moldavia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moldavia) and [Wallachia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wallachia) in the 6th century,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHeather2010395–397-52] in Transylvania around 600.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBóna199497–99-53] The nomadic [Avars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pannonian_Avars) defeated the Gepids and established a powerful empire around 570.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHitchins201416-44][^cite_note-FOOTNOTECurta200662–63-54] The [Bulgars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bulgars), who also came from the European [Pontic steppe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pontic_steppe), occupied the [Lower Danube](21209639.md) region in 680.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHitchins201416-44]



&#x20;[First Bulgarian Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Bulgarian_Empire) (681–1018) around 850

After the [Avar Khaganate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Avar_Khaganate) collapsed in the 790s, the [First Bulgarian Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Bulgarian_Empire) became the dominant power of the region, occupying lands as far as the river [Tisa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tisa).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHitchins201416-44] The [First Bulgarian Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Bulgarian_Empire) had a mixed population consisting of the [Bulgar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bulgars) conquerors, [Slavs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slavs) and [Vlachs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vlachs) (or Romanians) but the [Slavicisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slavicisation) of the Bulgar elite had already begun in the 9th century. Following the conquest of southern [Transylvania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transylvania) around 830, people from the Bulgar Empire mined salt at the local salt mines.[^cite_note-55] The [Council of Preslav](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Council_of_Preslav) declared [Old Church Slavonic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_Church_Slavonic) the language of liturgy in the country in 893.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECurta2006168,_177-56] The Vlachs also adopted Old Church Slavonic as their liturgical language.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHitchins201437-57]

The [Magyars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Magyars) (or [Hungarians](17106358.md)) took control of the steppes north of the Lower Danube in the 830s, but the [Bulgarians](151876.md) and the [Pechenegs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pechenegs) forced them to abandon this region for the [lowlands along the Middle Danube](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Hungarian_Plain) around 894.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECurta2006123,_178-58] Centuries later, the *[Gesta Hungarorum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gesta_Hungarorum)* wrote of the invading Magyars' wars against three dukes—[Glad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glad_\(duke\)), [Menumorut](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Menumorut) and the Vlach [Gelou](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gelou)—for [Banat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Banat), [Crișana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cri%C8%99ana) and [Transylvania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transylvania).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHitchins201420-59][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGeorgescu199114–15-60] The *Gesta* also listed many peoples—[Slavs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slavs), Bulgarians, [Vlachs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vlachs), [Khazars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khazars), and [Székelys](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sz%C3%A9kelys)—inhabiting the same regions.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESălăgean2005140-61][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPop199936-62] The reliability of the *Gesta* is debated. Some scholars regard it as a basically accurate account, others describe it as a literary work filled with invented details.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECurta200615–16_(note_41)-63][^cite_note-FOOTNOTESălăgean2005140–141-64][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBóna1994111-65] The Pechenegs seized the lowlands abandoned by the Hungarians to the east of the [Carpathians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carpathian_Mountains).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECurta2006182–183-66]

[Byzantine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine) missionaries proselytised in the lands east of the Tisa from the 940s[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECurta2006189–190-67] and [Byzantine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine_Empire) troops occupied [Dobruja](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dobruja) in the 970s.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESălăgean2005152-68] The first [king of Hungary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/King_of_Hungary), [Stephen I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stephen_I_of_Hungary), who supported [Western European](33800.md) missionaries, defeated the local chieftains and established [Roman Catholic bishoprics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_Church_in_Romania) (office of a bishop) in [Transylvania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transylvania) and [Banat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Banat) in the early 11th century.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECurta2006248–250-69][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPop199940–41-70] Significant Pecheneg groups fled to the Byzantine Empire in the 1040s; the [Oghuz Turks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oghuz_Turks) followed them, and the nomadic [Cumans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cumans) became the dominant power of the steppes in the 1060s.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECurta2006304–305-71] Cooperation between the Cumans and the [Vlachs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vlachs) against the Byzantine Empire is well documented from the end of the 11th century.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESălăgean2005157-72] Scholars who reject the [Daco-Roman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daco-Roman) continuity theory say that the first Vlach groups left their [Balkan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Balkan) homeland for the mountain pastures of the eastern and southern Carpathians in the 11th century, establishing the Romanians' presence in the lands to the north of the Lower Danube.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBóna1994183-73]



[Vlad III of Wallachia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vlad_III_of_Wallachia) (also known as Vlad the Impaler), medieval ruler of Wallachia

Exposed to nomadic incursions, Transylvania developed into an important border province of the [Kingdom of Hungary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Hungary).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESălăgean2005158–159-74][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBóna1994144–145-75] The Székelys—a community of free warriors—settled in [central Transylvania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transylvania) around 1100 and moved to the easternmost regions around 1200.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPop199943-76] Colonists from the [Holy Roman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holy_Roman_Empire)—the [Transylvanian Saxons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transylvanian_Saxons)' ancestors—came to the province in the 1150s.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPop199943-76][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHitchins201421-77] A high-ranking royal official, styled [voivode](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Voivode_of_Transylvania), ruled the Transylvanian [counties](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Counties_of_Hungary_\(before_1920\)) from the 1170s, but the [Székely](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sz%C3%A9kely_Land) and [Saxon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saxons) [seats](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seat_\(territorial_administrative_unit\)) (or districts) were not subject to the voivodes' authority.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPop199941–43-78] Royal charters wrote of the "[Vlachs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vlachs)' land" in southern Transylvania in the early 13th century, indicating the existence of [autonomous Romanian communities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romanian_district).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBóna1994189-79] Papal correspondence mentions the activities of Orthodox prelates among the Romanians in [Muntenia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muntenia) in the 1230s.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECurta2006408-80] Also in the 13th century, the [Republic of Genoa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republic_of_Genoa) started establishing [colonies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genoese_colonies) on the [Black Sea](3386.md), including [Calafat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Calafat), and [Constanța](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constan%C8%9Ba).[^cite_note-81][^cite_note-82]

The [Mongols](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mongols) destroyed large territories during [their invasion of Eastern and Central Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mongol_invasion_of_Europe) in 1241 and 1242.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPop199943–44-83] The Mongols' [Golden Horde](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Golden_Horde) emerged as the dominant power of Eastern Europe, but [Béla IV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/B%C3%A9la_IV) of [Hungary](13275.md)'s land grant to the [Knights Hospitallers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Knights_Hospitallers) in [Oltenia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oltenia) and [Muntenia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muntenia) shows that the local [Vlach](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vlach) rulers were subject to the king's authority in 1247.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTECurta2006407,_414-84][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPop199944-85] [Basarab I of Wallachia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Basarab_I_of_Wallachia) united the Romanian polities between the southern [Carpathians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carpathian_Mountains) and the [Lower Danube](21209639.md) in the 1310s.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPop199945-86] He defeated the Hungarian royal army in the [Battle of Posada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Posada) and secured the independence of [Wallachia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wallachia) in 1330.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPop199946-87][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGeorgescu199117-88] The second Romanian principality, [Moldavia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moldavia), achieved full autonomy during the reign of [Bogdan I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bogdan_I_of_Moldavia) around 1360.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGeorgescu199117-88] A local dynasty ruled the [Despotate of Dobruja](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Despotate_of_Dobruja) in the second half of the 14th century, but the [Ottoman Empire](22278.md) took possession of the territory after 1388.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTESălăgean2005202-89]

Princes [Mircea I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mircea_I_of_Wallachia) and [Vlad III of Wallachia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vlad_the_Impaler), and [Stephen III of Moldavia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stephen_the_Great) defended their countries' independence against the Ottomans. Most [Wallachian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wallachia) and [Moldavian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moldovans) princes paid a regular tribute to the [Ottoman sultans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_sultans_of_the_Ottoman_Empire) from 1417 and 1456, respectively.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHitchins201426–29-90][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPop199960–61,_63–66-91] [John Hunyadi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Hunyadi), organised the defence of the [Kingdom of Hungary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Hungary) and [anti-Ottoman campaigns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hungarian%E2%80%93Ottoman_Wars) from 1440 until his death in 1456.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPop199961–62-92] Increasing taxes outraged the [Transylvanian peasants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transylvanian_peasant_revolt), and they rose up in an open rebellion in 1437, but the [Hungarian nobles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hungarian_nobility) and the heads of the [Saxon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saxons) and [Székely](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sz%C3%A9kely_Land) communities jointly suppressed their revolt.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGeorgescu199130–31-93] The formal alliance of the [Hungarian](17106358.md), Saxon, and Székely leaders, known as the [Union of the Three Nations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unio_Trium_Nationum), became an important element of the self-government of [Transylvania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transylvania).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPop199952–53-94] The Orthodox Romanian *[knezes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Knez_\(Vlach_leader\))* ("chiefs") were excluded from the Union.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPop199952–53-94]

### Early modern times and national awakening

Main articles: [Early Modern Romania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_Modern_Romania) and [Romanian War of Independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romanian_War_of_Independence)





During the [Long Turkish War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Long_Turkish_War), [Wallachian Prince](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_rulers_of_Wallachia) [Michael the Brave](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_the_Brave) (*portrayed*) briefly reigned over the three medieval principalities of [Wallachia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wallachia), [Moldavia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moldavia), and [Transylvania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transylvania), covering most of the present-day territory of Romania

The [Kingdom of Hungary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Hungary) collapsed, and the [Ottomans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_Turks) occupied parts of [Banat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Banat) and [Crișana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cri%C8%99ana) in 1541.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPop199952–53-94] Transylvania and [Maramureș](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maramure%C8%99), along with the rest of Banat and Crișana, developed into a new state under Ottoman suzerainty, the [Principality of Transylvania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Principality_of_Transylvania_\(1570%E2%80%931711\)).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGeorgescu199141-95] The [Reformation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reformation), initiated in [Germany](11867.md) by [Martin Luther](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Martin_Luther) in 1517, encouraged the spread of [Protestantism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protestantism) across the region; by 1568, the [Edict of Torda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edict_of_Torda) formally granted local communities the right to choose their own preachers, sanctioning the existence of four "received" religions ([Catholicism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholicism), [Lutheranism](23371382.md), [Calvinism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Calvinism), and [Unitarianism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unitarian_Church_of_Transylvania).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPop199969-96] The Romanians' Orthodox faith remained only tolerated,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPop199969-96] although they made up more than one-third of the population, according to 17th-century estimates.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETrócsányiMiskolczy1994419-97][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGeorgescu199171-98] In June 1609, [Gabriel Báthory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gabriel_B%C3%A1thory) freed the Romanian Orthodox clergy from both taxation and service demands.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTENagy1988290-99]

The princes of [Transylvania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transylvania), [Wallachia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wallachia), and [Moldavia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moldavia) joined the [Holy League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holy_League_\(1594\)) against the [Ottoman Empire](22278.md) in 1594.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGeorgescu199154-100] The Wallachian prince, [Michael the Brave](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_the_Brave), united the three principalities under his rule in May 1600.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHitchins201435-101][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGeorgescu199155–56-102] The neighbouring powers forced him to abdicate in September, but he became a symbol of the unification of the Romanian lands in the 19th century.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHitchins201435-101] Although the rulers of the three principalities continued to pay tribute to the [Ottomans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_Turks), the most talented princes—[Gabriel Bethlen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gabriel_Bethlen) of Transylvania, [Matei Basarab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Matei_Basarab) of Wallachia, and [Vasile Lupu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vasile_Lupu) of Moldavia—strengthened their autonomy.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPop199975–76-103]

The united armies of the [Holy League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holy_League_\(1684\)) expelled the Ottoman troops from [Central Europe](5188.md) between 1684 and 1699, and the [Principality of Transylvania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Principality_of_Transylvania_\(1570%E2%80%931711\)) was integrated into the [Habsburg monarchy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Habsburg_monarchy).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPop199979-104] The [Habsburgs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/House_of_Habsburg) supported the Catholic clergy and persuaded the Orthodox Romanian prelates to accept the [union with the Roman Catholic Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Church_union) in 1699.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGeorgescu199142-105] In the 18th century, [Moldavia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moldavia) and [Wallachia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wallachia) maintained their internal autonomy, but in 1711 and 1716, respectively, the period of the [Phanariots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phanariots) began, with rulers appointed directly by the Porte from among the noble families of [Greek](42056.md) origin in [Constantinople](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constantinople). With the signing of the [Ausgleich](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ausgleich) in 1867, [Transylvania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transylvania) quickly lost its remaining political autonomy, being politically and administratively incorporated into the [Kingdom of Hungary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Hungary).[^cite_note-106] The Church Union strengthened the Romanian intellectuals' devotion to their Roman heritage.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHitchins201460-107] The Orthodox Church was restored in Transylvania only after Orthodox monks stirred up revolts in 1744 and 1759.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHitchins201461-108] The organisation of the [Transylvanian Military Frontier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transylvanian_Military_Frontier) caused further disturbances, [especially among the Székelys in 1764](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siculicidium).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETrócsányiMiskolczy1994432–434-109]

Princes [Dimitrie Cantemir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dimitrie_Cantemir) of Moldavia and [Constantin Brâncoveanu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constantin_Br%C3%A2ncoveanu) of [Wallachia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wallachia) concluded alliances with the [Habsburg Monarchy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Habsburg_monarchy) and [Russia](25391.md) against the [Ottomans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_Turks), but they were dethroned in 1711 and 1714, respectively.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHitchins201444–45-110] The sultans lost confidence in the native princes and appointed Orthodox merchants from the [Phanar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phanar) district of [Istanbul](3391396.md) to rule [Moldavia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moldavia) and Wallachia.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHitchins201455–56-111][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGeorgescu199173–74-112] The [Phanariot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phanariot) princes pursued oppressive fiscal policies and dissolved the army.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGeorgescu199174–75,_78-113] The neighbouring powers took advantage of the situation: the Habsburg Monarchy annexed the northwestern part of Moldavia, or [Bukovina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bukovina), in 1775, and the [Russian Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Empire) seized the eastern half of Moldavia, or [Bessarabia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bessarabia), in 1812.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPop199992-114][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGeorgescu199175–76-115]

A census revealed that the Romanians were more numerous than any other ethnic group in [Transylvania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transylvania) in 1733, but legislation continued to use contemptuous adjectives (such as "tolerated" and "admitted") when referring to them.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPop199987-116][^cite_note-FOOTNOTETrócsányiMiskolczy1994427–428-117] The [Uniate bishop](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romanian_Greek_Catholic_Church), [Inocențiu Micu-Klein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inocen%C8%9Biu_Micu-Klein), who demanded recognition of the Romanians as the fourth privileged nation, was forced into exile.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGeorgescu199189–90-118][^cite_note-FOOTNOTETrócsányiMiskolczy1994427–428-117] Uniate and Orthodox clerics and laymen jointly signed a [plea for the Transylvanian Romanians' emancipation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supplex_Libellus_Valachorum) in 1791, but the monarch and the local authorities refused to grant their requests.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHitchins201473–74-119][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPop199987-116]



Animated map depicting the territorial changes of Romania from [1859](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unification_of_Moldavia_and_Wallachia) to [2010](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maritime_Delimitation_in_the_Black_Sea_case)

The [Treaty of Küçük Kaynarca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_K%C3%BC%C3%A7%C3%BCk_Kaynarca) authorised the Russian ambassador in [Istanbul](3391396.md) to defend the autonomy of Moldavia and Wallachia (known as the [Danubian Principalities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Danubian_Principalities)) in 1774.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHitchins201468-120] Taking advantage of the [Greek War of Independence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_War_of_Independence), a Wallachian lesser nobleman, [Tudor Vladimirescu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tudor_Vladimirescu), stirred up a revolt against the Ottomans in January 1821, but he was murdered in June by [Phanariot Greeks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phanariots).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEGeorgescu1991103–104-121] After a [new Russo-Turkish War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russo-Turkish_War_\(1828%E2%80%9329\)), the [Treaty of Adrianople](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Adrianople_\(1829\)) strengthened the autonomy of the Danubian Principalities in 1829, although it also acknowledged the sultan's right to confirm the election of the princes.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHitchins201481-122]

[Mihail Kogălniceanu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mihail_Kog%C4%83lniceanu), [Nicolae Bălcescu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nicolae_B%C4%83lcescu) and other leaders of the [1848 revolutions in Moldavia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moldavian_Revolution_of_1848) and [Wallachia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wallachian_Revolution_of_1848) demanded the emancipation of the peasants and the union of the two principalities, but Russian and Ottoman troops crushed their revolt.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPop199999-123][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHitchins201496–97-124] The Wallachian revolutionaries were the first to adopt the blue, yellow and red [tricolour](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tricolour) as the [national flag](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flag_of_Romania), which included the motto "Justice, Fraternity" (*Dreptate, Frăție*) to symbolise the bond between Wallachians and Moldavians.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPop1999100-125] In Transylvania, most Romanians supported the imperial government against the [Hungarian revolutionaries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hungarian_Revolution_of_1848) after the Diet passed a law concerning the union of [Transylvania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transylvania) and [Hungary](13275.md).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEPop1999100-125] Bishop [Andrei Șaguna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andrei_%C8%98aguna) proposed the unification of the Romanians of the Habsburg Monarchy in a separate duchy, but the central government refused to change the internal borders.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHitchins201499-126]

### Unification and the Kingdom of Romania

Main articles: [Unification of Moldavia and Wallachia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unification_of_Moldavia_and_Wallachia), [United Principalities of Moldavia and Wallachia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Principalities_of_Moldavia_and_Wallachia), and [Kingdom of Romania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Romania)



[King Carol I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/King_Carol_I) with his nephew [Ferdinand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ferdinand_I_of_Romania) and his son, [Carol II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carol_II)

#### From the Little Union to the Great War

The modern Romanian state was created through the [unification of the principalities of Moldavia and Wallachia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unification_of_the_Romanian_Principalities), accepted as a federative structure by the [Great Powers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_balance_of_power) following the [Paris Convention](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paris_Convention) of 1858, and later cemented by the simultaneous election as ruler of both states of the unionist [Alexandru Ioan Cuza](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexandru_Ioan_Cuza).[^cite_note-150_2012-07-06-127][^cite_note-128][^cite_note-129] After carrying out numerous reforms that laid the foundations for the modernisation of the state, he was forced in 1866 by a broad coalition of the political parties of the time, also known as the "[Monstrous coalition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monstrous_coalition)", to abdicate and leave the country.[^cite_note-FC-130]

The union was at one time in peril, but the political leaders of the era succeeded in placing on the princely throne [Carol I of Romania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carol_I_of_Romania), who accepted the Constitution and took the oath on 10 May 1866. Eleven years later, on 10 May 1877, Romania proclaimed its independence—achieved on the battlefield—and in 1881, on the same day of the year, Carol was crowned as [King of Romania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/King_of_Romania). In 1913, Romania entered the [Second Balkan War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Balkan_War) against [Bulgaria](3415.md), at the end of which it obtained [Southern Dobruja](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Dobruja).[^cite_note-131][^cite_note-132][^cite_note-133] In 1914, [King Carol I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/King_Carol_I) died, and his nephew, [Ferdinand I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ferdinand_I_of_Romania), succeeded him on the throne.[^cite_note-FC-130]

#### World War I and the Great Union

Main article: [Romania in World War I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romania_in_World_War_I)

In 1916, Romania entered [World War I](4764461.md) on the side of the [Entente Powers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Entente_Powers).[^cite_note-134] Although the Romanian forces did not perform well militarily, by the end of the war the [Austrian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Austrian_Empire) and [Russian Empires](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Empire) had disintegrated; the [National Assembly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_National_Assembly_of_Alba_Iulia) in [Transylvania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transylvania), and the [Sfatul Țării](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sfatul_%C8%9A%C4%83rii) in [Bessarabia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bessarabia) and [Bukovina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bukovina) proclaimed their union with Romania, and [King Ferdinand I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ferdinand_I_of_Romania) and [Queen Maria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Queen_Maria_of_Romania) were crowned sovereign of all Romanians in [Alba Iulia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alba_Iulia) on 15 October 1922.[^cite_note-135]

After [World War I](4764461.md), the union of [Bukovina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bukovina) with Romania was ratified in 1919 by the [Treaty of Saint Germain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Saint-Germain-en-Laye_\(1919\)). Most of the territories claimed by Romania from the [Kingdom of Hungary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Hungary)—[Crișana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cri%C8%99ana), [Transylvania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transylvania) and parts of [Banat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Banat) and [Maramureș](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maramure%C8%99)—were annexed to the [Kingdom of Romania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Romania). This act was ratified in 1920 by the [Treaty of Trianon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Trianon), which defined the new border between [Hungary](13275.md) and Romania.

#### Interwar period

After having left the country and renounced his claim to the throne in 1925, [Carol II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carol_II) returned in 1930 and usurped his son's throne; influenced by his inner circle—referred to by historians as the "Royal Camarilla"—he gradually undermined the democratic system, and in 1938 he assumed dictatorial powers. Although he was pro-Western (especially Anglophile), Carol attempted to appease extreme centrifugal forces by appointing nationalist governments that adopted anti-Semitic measures, such as the [Goga cabinet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Goga_cabinet) and the one led by the Orthodox Patriarch [Miron Cristea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miron_Cristea).

#### World War II

Main article: [Romania in World War II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romania_in_World_War_II)



Romania's territorial losses in the summer of 1940. Of these territories, only [Northern Transylvania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_Transylvania) was [regained](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paris_Peace_Treaties,_1947) after the end of World War II

Following the [Molotov–Ribbentrop Pact](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Molotov%E2%80%93Ribbentrop_Pact) of 1939, in June 1940 Romania accepted the loss of [Bessarabia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bessarabia), [Northern Bukovina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_Bukovina) and the [Hertsa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hertsa) region in favour of the [USSR](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/USSR) (as stipulated in the Soviet ultimatum of 28 June 1940). Unaware of the details of the Soviet–German pact, Carol attempted to secure an alliance with [Nazi Germany](21212.md), and appointed [Ion Gigurtu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ion_Gigurtu) as President of the Council of Ministers, who declared that he would pursue a [Nazi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nazism) pro-Axis ([Berlin](3354.md)–[Rome](25458.md)) policy that was anti-Semitic and fascist-totalitarian in nature.[^cite_note-136][^cite_note-137][^cite_note-138] Between 4 July and 4 September 1940, by accepting [Hitler's arbitration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Vienna_Award) over [Transylvania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transylvania) (after Gigurtu declared on radio that Romania must make territorial sacrifices to justify its Nazi orientation and full adherence to the Berlin–Rome Axis), Romania ceded [Northern Transylvania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_Transylvania)—including the city of [Cluj](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cluj)—to Hungary.[^cite_note-139][^cite_note-140][^cite_note-141] The vast territories in Transylvania ceded by Ion Gigurtu to Hungary contained important natural resources, including gold mines.[^cite_note-142] Ion Gigurtu also initiated negotiations to cede 8,000 km<sup>2</sup> of [Southern Dobruja](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Dobruja) to [Bulgaria](3415.md),[^cite_note-143] these negotiations were interrupted by Antonescu's unconditional acceptance of the territorial cession.[^cite_note-romlib2018-07-04-144]

In response to the chaotic withdrawal from [Bessarabia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bessarabia), the territorial cessions, public discontent, and protests from political leaders, [King Carol II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carol_II_of_Romania) suspended the [1938 Constitution of Romania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1938_Constitution_of_Romania) and appointed General [Ion Antonescu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ion_Antonescu) as Prime Minister. This measure, supported by the [Iron Guard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iron_Guard), demanded that the king abdicate in favour of his son, [Mihai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mihai_I_of_Romania). Subsequently, Antonescu assumed dictatorial powers and became President of the Council of Ministers, as the self-titled "Leader" of the state.[^cite_note-barba-145][^cite_note-Encarta-146]

In 1941, as an ally of [Nazi Germany](21212.md), Romania entered [World War II](32927.md) by declaring war on the [Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Union).[^cite_note-barba-145][^cite_note-Encarta-146] A shift in fortunes only became discernible after the defeat at [Stalingrad](32585.md) and the subsequent change of the USSR from a defensive to an offensive posture. On 23 August 1944, with the Soviet army having been present in [northern Moldova](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moldavian_Plain) since March, [King Mihai I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mihai_I_of_Romania) forcibly removed Marshal [Ion Antonescu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ion_Antonescu) from power, as he refused to sign an armistice with the [Allies of World War II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allies_of_World_War_II).[^cite_note-Memoria-147] Following Antonescu's outright refusal, King Mihai I ordered the dismissal and arrest of the marshal, and Romania switched sides to join the Allies.[^cite_note-Memoria-147]

### Socialist Romania (1947–1989)

Main article: [Socialist Republic of Romania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Socialist_Republic_of_Romania)



[Nicolae Ceaușescu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nicolae_Ceau%C8%99escu) with [Kim Il Sung](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kim_Il_Sung) of [North Korea](21255.md) in 1978. In [reforming the state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/July_Theses), Ceaușescu sought to emulate *[Juche](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juche)* and [Maoist](46636.md) ideas

[^cite_note-Cioroianu_489-148]

[^cite_note-Liiceanu_xviii-149]

[^cite_note-Tismăneanu_241-150]

Less than three years after the [Soviet occupation of Romania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_occupation_of_Romania), in 1947, [King Michael I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/King_Michael_I) was forced to abdicate[^cite_note-151] and the [People's Republic of Romania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/People's_Republic_of_Romania)—a state of "popular democracy"—was proclaimed. The newly established communist regime, led by the [Romanian Workers' Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romanian_Workers'_Party), consolidated its power through a Stalinist-type policy aimed at suppressing any political opposition and transforming the economic and social structures of the old bourgeois regime.[^cite_note-CPADR-152][^cite_note-153]

In the early 1960s, the Romanian government began asserting a certain degree of independence from the [Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Union) in its foreign policy,[^cite_note-ICIistoria-154] although it did not abandon its repressive policies (which it labelled "revolutionary conquests") in domestic affairs.[^cite_note-CPADR-152] In 1965, communist leader [Gheorghe Gheorghiu-Dej](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gheorghe_Gheorghiu-Dej) died, ushering in a period of change in Romania.[^cite_note-HisBritan-155] After a brief power struggle, [Nicolae Ceaușescu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nicolae_Ceau%C8%99escu) emerged as the head of the communist party,[^cite_note-HisBritan-155] becoming General Secretary of the [Romanian Communist Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romanian_Communist_Party) in 1965, President of the [State Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_Council_of_Romania) in 1967, and President of the [Socialist Republic of Romania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Socialist_Republic_of_Romania) in 1974. Ceaușescu's rule from 1965 to 1989 grew increasingly authoritarian during the 1980s.[^cite_note-ICIistoria-154]

### Romania since 1989

Main article: [Romanian revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romanian_revolution)



Tanks and [Miliția](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mili%C8%9Bia_\(Romania\)) on the [Magheru Boulevard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Magheru_Boulevard) in [Bucharest](36877.md) during the revolution

In the context of the fall of communism throughout [Eastern Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Europe) during the [revolutions of 1989](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Revolutions_of_1989), a protest in support of [Reformed pastor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reformed_Church_in_Romania) [László Tőkés](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/L%C3%A1szl%C3%B3_T%C5%91k%C3%A9s) that began in December 1989 in [Timișoara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timi%C8%99oara) quickly escalated into a national uprising against the [communist regime](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Socialist_Republic_of_Romania), ultimately resulting in the [execution of Ceaușescu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trial_and_execution_of_Nicolae_and_Elena_Ceau%C8%99escu) and his wife [Elena](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elena_Ceau%C8%99escu) on 25 December 1989.[^cite_note-156]

An interim council composed of figures from civil society and former communist officials assumed control of the government, and [Ion Iliescu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ion_Iliescu) became the provisional president of the country. The new government reversed many of the authoritarian communist policies[^cite_note-157][^cite_note-158][^cite_note-159] and dismissed several leaders of the former regime, although still influenced by members of the former regime (the basis for the [Golaniad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Golaniad)*,* and [Mineriads](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mineriads)).

In [May 1990](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1990_Romanian_general_election), the first free elections in Romania since the [1937](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1937_Romanian_general_election) elections were held, with Iliescu of the [National Salvation Front](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Salvation_Front_\(Romania\)) winning the presidency with 85% of the vote. In [1992](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1992_Romanian_general_election), he was reelected in the first election after the adoption of [a permanent constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_Romania) via [a referendum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1991_Romanian_constitutional_referendum) held the previous year. [Illiescu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ion_Iliescu) lost the [1996 election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1996_Romanian_general_election) to [Emil Constantinescu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emil_Constantinescu), but returned to power in [2000](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2000_Romanian_general_election). [Traian Băsescu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Traian_B%C4%83sescu) was elected president in [2004](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2004_Romanian_general_election) and [2009](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2009_Romanian_presidential_election), serving until [2014](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_Romanian_presidential_election) at which point [Klaus Iohannis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Klaus_Iohannis) succeeded him, being re-elected in [2019](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_Romanian_presidential_election) and serving until [2025](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025_Romanian_presidential_election). During these years several events occurred. In 2009, the country was bailed out by the [International Monetary Fund](15251.md) as result of the [Great Recession in Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Recession_in_Europe) following the [2008 financial crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2008_financial_crisis).[^cite_note-igber-160]

The post-1989 period has been characterised by the privatisation and closure of several former industrial and economic enterprises from the [communist period](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Socialist_Republic_of_Romania),[^cite_note-161] while [corruption](41107761.md) has been a major issue in contemporary politics.[^cite_note-162]

A [National Anticorruption Directorate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Anticorruption_Directorate) was formed in the country in 2002.[^cite_note-163] During the 2000s, Romania had one of the [highest economic growth rates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economy_of_Romania) in [Europe](9239.md) and has been referred at times as "the Tiger of Eastern Europe".[^cite_note-164] This has been accompanied by a significant improvement in [living standards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Standard_of_living) as the country successfully reduced domestic [poverty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Poverty) and established a functional democratic state.[^cite_note-hdrstats.undp.org-165][^cite_note-166] However, Romania's development suffered a major setback during the [late 2000s' recession](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Recession) leading to a large [gross domestic product](12594.md) contraction and a budget deficit in 2009.[^cite_note-wsj4dec09-167] This led to Romania borrowing from the [International Monetary Fund](15251.md).[^cite_note-168] Worsening economic conditions led to [unrest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2012_Romanian_constitutional_crisis) and triggered a political crisis in 2012.[^cite_note-169]

Since 2014, Romania launched an anti-corruption effort that led to the prosecution of medium- and high-level political, judicial and administrative offences by the [National Anticorruption Directorate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Anticorruption_Directorate).[^cite_note-The_US_Department_of_State-170] In 2015, massive [anti-corruption protests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2015_Romanian_protests) which developed in the wake of the [Colectiv nightclub fire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colectiv_nightclub_fire) led to the resignation of prime minister [Victor Ponta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Victor_Ponta).[^cite_note-171] During 2017–2019, in response to measures which were perceived to weaken the fight against [corruption](3608404.md), some of the [biggest post-1989 protests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2017%E2%80%932019_Romanian_protests) took place in Romania, with over 500,000 people protesting nationwide.[^cite_note-172][^cite_note-173][^cite_note-The_US_Department_of_State-170]

The [2021 Romanian political crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_Romanian_political_crisis) led to the ousting of [Florin Cîțu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Florin_C%C3%AE%C8%9Bu)'s [incumbent government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/C%C3%AE%C8%9Bu_Cabinet). The [Ciucă Cabinet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ciuc%C4%83_Cabinet) then took power, with Romania since having experienced a shift towards authoritarianism and [illiberalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Illiberalism),[^cite_note-174][^cite_note-175][^cite_note-176][^cite_note-177] as well as an increased [corruption](41107761.md). Around this time, Romania was also hit by the [COVID-19 pandemic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic_in_Romania). In the [2024 presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Romanian_presidential_election), Independent candidate [Călin Georgescu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/C%C4%83lin_Georgescu) achieved a surprise win in the first round. However, the [Constitutional Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitutional_Court_of_Romania) annulled the election results, citing [Russian meddling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Accusations_of_Russian_interference_in_the_2024_Romanian_presidential_election). The cancellation led to [far-right protests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024%E2%80%932025_Romanian_election_annulment_protests), [criticism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025_JD_Vance_speech_at_the_Munich_Security_Conference) by the [Trump administration](78298108.md), and [Ilie Bolojan](78639092.md) becoming acting president in February 2025 as Iohannis resigned to political pressure.[^cite_note-Chao-Fong-178] In the subsequent [2025 Romanian presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025_Romanian_presidential_election), [Bucharest Mayor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mayor_of_Bucharest) [Nicușor Dan](35543359.md) was elected [president](273659.md).

## Geography

Main article: [Geography of Romania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geography_of_Romania)



Topographic map of Romania

Romania is the largest country in [Southeastern Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southeast_Europe) and the [twelfth-largest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_European_countries_by_area) in [Europe](9239.md), having an area of 238,397 square kilometres (92,046 sq mi).[^cite_note-yearbook-179]<sup>: 17 </sup> It lies between latitudes [43°](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/43rd_parallel_north) and [49° N](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/49%C2%B0_N) and longitudes [20°](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/20th_meridian_east) and [30° E](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/30th_meridian_east). The terrain is distributed roughly equally between [mountains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mountain), [hills](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hill), and [plains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plain). The [Carpathian Mountains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carpathian_Mountains) dominate the centre of Romania, with [14 mountain ranges](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_mountain_peaks_in_Romania) reaching above 2,000 m or 6,600 ft—the highest is [Moldoveanu Peak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moldoveanu_Peak) at 2,544 m or 8,346 ft.[^cite_note-yearbook-179]<sup>: 11 </sup> They are surrounded by the [Moldavian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moldavian_Plateau) and [Transylvanian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transylvanian_Plateau) plateaus, the [Pannonian Plain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pannonian_Plain) and the [Wallachian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wallachian_Plain) plains.

Romania is home to six terrestrial ecoregions: [Balkan mixed forests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Balkan_mixed_forests), [Central European mixed forests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_European_mixed_forests), [East European forest steppe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_European_forest_steppe), [Pannonian mixed forests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pannonian_mixed_forests), [Carpathian montane conifer forests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carpathian_montane_conifer_forests), and [Pontic steppe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pontic_steppe).[^cite_note-DinersteinOlson2017-180] Natural and semi-natural ecosystems cover about 47% of the country's land area.[^cite_note-biodiversity-181] There are almost 10,000 km<sup>2</sup> (3,900 sq mi) (about 5% of the total area) of [protected areas in Romania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protected_areas_in_Romania) covering 13 [national parks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_park) and three [biosphere](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biosphere) reserves.[^cite_note-182] The [Danube](21209639.md) river forms a large part of the border with [Serbia](29265.md) and [Bulgaria](3415.md), and flows into the [Black Sea](3386.md), forming the Danube Delta, which is the second-largest and best-preserved delta in Europe, and a [biosphere reserve](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biosphere_reserve) and a biodiversity [World Heritage Site](44940.md).[^cite_note-183] At 5,800 km<sup>2</sup> (2,200 sq mi),[^cite_note-184] the [Danube Delta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Danube_Delta) is the largest continuous marshland in [Europe](9239.md),[^cite_note-UNESCO-185] and supports 1,688 different [plant species](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flora) alone.[^cite_note-186]

Romania has one of the largest areas of undisturbed forest in Europe, covering almost 27% of its territory.[^cite_note-187] The country had a 2019 [Forest Landscape Integrity Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forest_Landscape_Integrity_Index) mean score of 5.95/10, ranking it 90th globally out of 172 countries.[^cite_note-FLII-Supplementary-188] Some 3,700 [plant species](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plant_species) have been identified in the country, from which to date 23 have been declared [natural monuments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natural_monument), 74 extinct, 39 endangered, 171 vulnerable, and 1,253 rare.[^cite_note-flora-189]

The [fauna of Romania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fauna_of_Romania) consists of 33,792 species of animals, 33,085 [invertebrate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Invertebrate) and 707 [vertebrate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vertebrate),[^cite_note-flora-189] with almost 400 unique species of [mammals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mammal), [birds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bird), [reptiles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reptile), and [amphibians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amphibian),[^cite_note-190] including about 50% of Europe's (excluding Russia) [brown bears](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brown_bear)[^cite_note-carnivoreconservation.org-191] and 20% of its [wolves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wolves).[^cite_note-192]

Romania is often seen by most as having the shape resembling one of a goldfish.

### Climate

Main article: [Climate of Romania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_of_Romania)



Romanian map of Köppen climate classification

Owing to its distance from open sea and its position on the southeastern portion of the European continent, Romania has a climate that is [continental](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Continental_climate), with four distinct seasons. The average annual temperature is 11 °C (52 °F) in the south and 8 °C (46 °F) in the north.[^cite_note-climate-193] In summer, average maximum temperatures in Bucharest rise to 28 °C (82 °F), and temperatures over 35 °C (95 °F) are fairly common in the lower-lying areas of the country.[^cite_note-clic.npolar.no-194] In winter, the average maximum temperature is below 2 °C (36 °F).[^cite_note-clic.npolar.no-194] Precipitation is average, with over 750 mm (30 in) per year only on the highest western mountains, while around Bucharest it drops to approximately 570 mm (22 in).[^cite_note-yearbook-179]<sup>: 29 </sup> There are some regional differences: in western sections, such as [Banat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Banat), the climate is milder and has some [Mediterranean influences](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Humid_continental_climate); the eastern part of the country has a more pronounced continental climate. In [Dobruja](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dobruja), the [Black Sea](3386.md) also exerts an influence over the region's climate.[^cite_note-195]

## Politics

Main article: [Politics of Romania](25639.md)



[Nicușor Dan](35543359.md)\
[President](273659.md)



[Ilie Bolojan](78639092.md)\
[Prime Minister](266262.md)

Romania is a [unitary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unitary_state) [semi-presidential](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Semi-presidential) [representative democratic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Representative_democratic) republic with a structured system of governance and an active civil society. The [President](273659.md), elected by popular vote, serves as the [head of state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Head_of_state), representing the country in [international affairs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_relations_of_Romania), safeguarding [constitutional order](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_Romania), and acting as [supreme commander](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commander-in-chief) of the [Romanian Armed Forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romanian_Armed_Forces). The [Prime Minister](266262.md), appointed by the President and confirmed by the [Parliament](4462971.md), acts as the [head of government](37570.md), responsible for overseeing the [executive branch](6046282.md), implementing domestic and foreign policies, and managing [public administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_administration). [Legislative authority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Legislative) is vested in a [bicameral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bicameral) Parliament, consisting of the [Chamber of Deputies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chamber_of_Deputies_of_Romania) and the [Senate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Senate_of_Romania), whose members are elected through a [proportional representation](23298.md) system. The [judiciary](2671545.md) operates [independently](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judicial_independence), with the [High Court of Cassation and Justice](1279550.md) as the highest court of appeal.

According to [International IDEA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_IDEA)'s Global State of Democracy (GSoD) Indices and Democracy Tracker, Romania performs in the mid- range on overall democratic measures, with particular strengths in inclusive suffrage and elected government.[^cite_note-196][^cite_note-197][^cite_note-198]

### Government

Main article: [Government of Romania](6046282.md)

Romania has a democratic, [multi-party system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multi-party_system), with [legislative power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Legislative_power) vested in the government and the [two chambers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bicameralism) of the [Parliament](4462971.md), more specifically the [Chamber of Deputies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chamber_of_Deputies_\(Romania\)) and the [Senate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Senate_of_Romania). The [judiciary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judiciary) is [independent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judicial_independence) of the executive and the legislature. The latter is elected by popular vote for a maximum of two terms of five years and appoints the prime minister who in turn appoints the [Council of Ministers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romanian_Cabinet). The legislative branch of the government, collectively known as the Parliament (residing at the [Palace of the Parliament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palace_of_the_Parliament)), consists of two chambers (Senate and Chamber of Deputies) whose members are elected every four years by [simple plurality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plurality_voting_system).[^cite_note-Europaworld-199][^cite_note-200]

The justice system is independent of the other branches of government and is made up of a hierarchical system of courts with the [High Court of Cassation and Justice](1279550.md) being the supreme court of Romania.[^cite_note-201] There are also courts of appeal, county courts and local courts. The Romanian judicial system is strongly influenced by the [French model](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_law), is based on [civil law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civil_law_\(legal_system\)) and is [inquisitorial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inquisitorial) in nature. The [Constitutional Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Curtea_Constitu%C8%9Bional%C4%83) (*Curtea Constituțională*) is responsible for judging the compliance of laws and other state regulations with the constitution, which is the fundamental law of the country and can be amended only through a [public referendum](51582.md).[^cite_note-Europaworld-199][^cite_note-202] Romania's 2007 entry into the EU has been a significant influence on its domestic policy, and including [judicial reforms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judicial_reform), increased judicial cooperation with other member states, and measures to combat corruption.[^cite_note-203] The [Economist Intelligence Unit](15303116.md) rated Romania as a "[flawed democracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flawed_democracy)" in 2025.[^cite_note-204]

### Administrative divisions

Main articles: [Administrative divisions of Romania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Administrative_divisions_of_Romania) and [Historical regions of Romania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Historical_regions_of_Romania)

Romania is divided into 41 [counties](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Counties_of_Romania) (*județe*) and the municipality of [Bucharest](36877.md). Each county is administered by a county council, responsible for local affairs, as well as a [prefect](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prefect_\(Romania\)) responsible for the administration of national affairs at the county level. The prefect is appointed by the central government but cannot be a member of any political party.[^cite_note-descopera-205] Each county is subdivided further into [cities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cities_of_Romania) and [communes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communes_of_Romania), which have their own mayor and local council. There are a total of 320 cities and 2,861 communes in Romania.[^cite_note-yearbook-179]<sup>: 17 </sup> A total of 103 of the larger cities have [municipality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Municipalities_of_Romania) status, which gives them greater administrative power over local affairs. The municipality of Bucharest is a special case, as it enjoys a status on par to that of a county. It is further divided into six [sectors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sectors_of_Bucharest)[^cite_note-yearbook-179]<sup>: 6 </sup> and has a prefect, a general mayor (*primar general*), and a general city council.

The NUTS-3 ([Nomenclature of Territorial Units for Statistics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nomenclature_of_Territorial_Units_for_Statistics)) level divisions of the EU reflect Romania's administrative-territorial structure and correspond to the [41 counties](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Counties_of_Romania) plus Bucharest.[^cite_note-nuts-206] The cities and communes correspond to the NUTS-5 level divisions, but there are no current NUTS-4 level divisions. The NUTS-1 (four [macroregions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Macroregions_of_Romania)) and NUTS-2[^cite_note-LEGE_nr.151_din_15_iulie_1998-207] (eight [development regions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Development_regions_of_Romania)) divisions exist but have no administrative capacity and are used instead for coordinating regional development projects and statistical purposes.[^cite_note-nuts-206]
